市場焦點
美國
公債回購
• 美國財政部9月9日宣布，將於翌日回購最高60億美元的10至20年期美債，低於部分市場期待
的80億至100億美元規模 。民間持有美債規模達 31.8兆美元，相較之下 ，本次60億美元回購
僅約占民間持債規模 0.02%，對整體影響有限 。此外，約6成的美債期限不足 5年，意味未來
再融資需求仍高，若財政赤字未明顯收斂，長端殖利率上行壓力仍存。
日本央行
• 日本第二季GDP年化季增率上修至 1.4%(優於初值1.1%)，儘管伊朗戰爭引發原油供應緊張給
經濟帶來壓力，但GDP增速依舊高於0.7%潛在經濟成長率，雖然經濟表現支持日本央行(BOJ)
逐步實施貨幣政策正常化 ，但更關鍵的催化劑來自日美兩國協調干預匯市 ，遏制日圓極端貶
值的政治壓力，彭博經濟研究預期日本央行將在9月會議上加息25個基點。

美國
通膨
• 美國8月生產者物價指數 （PPI）年增5.4%，略高市場預期的 5.3%，核心PPI年增4.6%，符合
預期。8月消費者物價指數（CPI）年增3.35%、核心CPI年增2.4%，均符合市場預期，但核心
CPI月升幅0.3%，高於市場預期的 0.2%。數據公佈後，FedWatch顯示市場預期9月升息機率，
從前一周的50%攀升至86%。

美國8月通膨數據(CPI)
• 美國8月通膨數據：8月美國CPI月增0.4%、年增3.4%，核心CPI月增0.3%，核心服務通膨升溫至0.5%，顯示通膨降溫仍不
均衡，能源價格上漲為主要推手，市場預估8月核心PCE月增約0.3%、年增約3.8%，仍高於Fed 2%目標，搭配強勁就業數
據，9月升息1碼的壓力升高。不過，本次通膨主要來自能源、需求及AI投資帶動，與2021～2022年供給型通膨有所不同。
• 市場反應與展望：CPI公布後，2年期美債殖利率一度上升，惟長天期殖利率反而走低，顯示市場關注焦點並非單純升息，而
是Fed能否維持抗通膨政策可信度。整體而言，升息未必等同Risk-off，若經濟與企業獲利維持韌性，股市仍具支撐。
資料來源： investing.com，2026.9.14

能源成本回升推升PPI，通膨壓力牽動Fed政策空間
• 能源成本回升，PPI重新升溫：美國8月PPI月增0.4%、年增5.4%，增幅均明顯高於7月，主要受到商品價格上漲帶動；其
中最終需求商品價格月增1.1%，能源價格上升4.2%，柴油更是大漲逾2成，成為商品價格上升的重要來源，惟最終需求服
務價格僅略增0.1%，顯示本次PPI升溫仍以能源及商品價格為主。
• 成本壓力仍在，利率政策不確定性升高： 扣除食品、能源及貿易服務後的核心 PPI月增0.3%，微低於7月的0.4%，但年增
4.7%，顯示價格壓力仍未完全消退；其中運輸與倉儲服務價格上升2.3%，反映部分成本仍具黏著性，致使市場對Fed利率
政策的重新定價明顯升溫，進而推動股、債市短線波動擴大。
• 市場觀察：由於本次PPI升溫主要來自能源成本 ，但服務價格壓力整體仍相對溫和 ，因此尚不足以視為通膨全面升溫 ，然

能源價格若持續走高 ，將提高Fed維持較高利率的壓力 ，建議投資人短線宜降低長天期債券與高估值成長股的追價 ，債券
可優先配置短中天期、高品質債券，股票則可關注能源、金融及具定價能力企業，提高投組對利率與成本波動的承受度。
資料來源：U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics，Producer Price Indexes – Aug,  2026，2026.9.11
季調後PPI最終需求價格指數月變動
資料來源：Bloomberg， 2026.9.11

• 財政部對美債的回購規模不如預期：美國財政部9月9日宣布，將於翌日回購最高60億美元的10至20年期美債，主要鎖定流

動性較低的舊券，以改善長天期債券市場流動性。雖然本次回購高於先前設定的每次至少40億美元，但低於部分市場期待的
80億至100億美元規模。消息公布後10年期公債殖利率逼近5%，30年期殖利率亦突破5.3%。
• 回購規模在高額赤字背景下，難以有效拉低長天期公債殖利率：美國今年公債發行規模較2025年增加11.8%，民間持有美債
規模達31.8兆美元、年增8.2%，相較之下，本次60億美元回購僅約占民間持債規模0.02%，對整體影響有限。此外，約6成
的美債期限不足5年，意味未來再融資需求仍高，若財政赤字未明顯收斂，長端殖利率上行壓力仍存。反觀新興國家基本面
持續改善，且當地債市有貨幣潛在升值空間，可作為全球債券配置的分散來源。

地緣政治動盪推升油價，但全球經濟依然展現韌性
市場共識未來12 個月預期每股盈餘

全球主要市場及國家經濟成長率
新興市場
美股
日股
歐股
• 地緣政治動盪推升油價，但全球經濟依然展現韌性：美國和伊朗的衝突已經進入第7個月，能源價格上升及地緣政治風險仍
對全球經濟與市場信心形成壓力，但AI投資熱潮持續推升科技需求，使全球經濟展現優於預期的韌性。其中，亞洲仍是全球
成長動能最強的區域之一，2026年亞洲（不含日本）經濟成長率預估達4.7%，明顯高於全球平均2.9%及成熟市場1.6%。在
內需與科技出口支撐下，新興亞洲基本面相對穩健，具備較佳的中長期配置條件。

• AI資本支出延續，台韓成為核心受惠市場：AI資本支出持續擴張，2026年全球雲端AI資本支出預估達7,960億美元，並帶動
AI半導體市場至2030年突破7,500億美元。亞洲掌握全球半導體供應鏈核心，台灣受惠於先進製程與先進封裝需求，韓國則
受惠於HBM記憶體需求，台韓可望成為AI基礎建設擴張的主要受惠市場；同時，中國與印度則提供較穩定的內需及經濟成
長支撐。2026年亞洲企業盈餘成長率預估達72.13%，顯著高於全球平均，建議新興亞洲以台韓科技股為主要成長配置，並
搭配中國、印度市場分散區域風險。

顧
短債供給增加未引發融資緊張，短天期債券防禦價值提升
• 回購市場維持穩定，短債供給暫未造成壓力：美國回購市場今年以來維持平穩，儘管9月短債償付與企業繳稅可能帶來短
暫資金波動，但SOFR與IORB利差仍處相對正常區間 ，顯示目前準備金與短期流動性尚稱充裕 。此外SOFR/IORB差值對
短期美債淨發行增加的敏感度明顯低於2025年底，反映流動性環境偏寬鬆。
• 貨幣基金成長放緩，供給吸收能力受到限制：零售資金流入偏弱，使貨幣市場基金資產增速落後 ，2026年迄今僅增加約
325億美元，遠低於2025年同期的2,050億美元；即便Q4季節性回升，新增資金對短債供給的承接能力仍可能有限。
• 後市觀察：年底前短債淨發行規模預估約3,250億美元，後續需關注短債供給、企業繳稅及財政部長端公債回購對資金面

的影響。建議投資人配置上可提高 1～3年期美國國庫券或短天期美債 ETF比重，以取得票息收益並降低長端殖利率波動
對債券價格的影響。
資料來源：US Treasury，FRBNY ，Federal Reserve ，2026.9.9
美債淨供應 vs. SOFR/IORB差值
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.9

風險偏好升溫與債券供給增加，推升期限溢價與長債殖利率
• 全球期限溢酬上升，長債面臨重新定價：全球債券殖利率同步走高，不僅反映各國政策利率預期，也受到全球期限溢酬上升
推動；投資人對持有長天期政府債券要求更高補償 ，顯示長債殖利率上行已具有跨市場的結構性因素 。Bloomberg
Economics的模型亦顯示，全球期限溢酬是近年G7長債殖利率走高的重要共同因素。
• 風險資產吸引資金，放大長債供需壓力：AI投資題材推升股票市場、信用利差仍相對偏窄，加上大宗商品走強，使股票、公
司債及商品與政府債券形成資金競爭；在政府融資需求持續增加的情況下 ，若債券需求未同步擴張，政府公債需提供更高殖
利率才能吸引資金。
• 後市觀察：長端利率已成為股債配置的重要變數 ，若殖利率因財政供給與風險偏好維持高檔 ，長天期債券及高估值股票均可

能承壓；建議以短中天期美債及高品質投資級公司債為核心 ，待長端殖利率充分反映期限溢酬後，再分批拉長債券存續期間。
資料來源：Bloomberg Economics，2026.9.9

日本央行升息箭在弦上，但缺乏連續升息條件
• GDP修正支持升息 ，但關鍵點在配合日本和美國政府遏制日圓疲軟： 日本第二季 GDP年化季增率上修至 1.4%(優於初值
1.1%)，儘管伊朗戰爭引發原油供應緊張給經濟帶來壓力，但GDP增速依舊高於0.7%潛在經濟成長率，雖然經濟表現支持日
本央行(BOJ)逐步實施貨幣政策正常化 ，但更關鍵的催化劑來自日美兩國協調干預匯市 ，遏制日圓極端貶值的政治壓力 ，彭
博經濟研究預期日本央行將在9月會議上加息25個基點。
• 缺乏連續大幅加息的支撐力道： (1)經濟衝擊風險：日圓升值效應已對經濟產生緊縮效果 ，彭博預估可能拖累實際 GDP成長
達0.3%。(2)財政負擔過重：若政策利率升至 1.75%以上，將大幅增加政府利息支出 ，擠壓高市內閣的財政刺激空間 。(3)企

業投資放緩：私人資本支出仍顯疲軟(季比下降0.9%)，顯示經濟復甦基礎尚未完全穩固。
• 小結：日本央行在政治、匯率與經濟基本面交集下 ，9月升息已成定局，但考量財政負擔與全球金融穩定後 ，其後續加息步
伐應偏向謹慎與循序漸進 ，密切關注9月日本央行會議後聲明 ，若央行強調循序漸進而非連續緊縮 ，市場波動相對可控；反
之，若釋放過度鷹派訊號，則需防範市場拋售風險。由於市場資金對日圓套利交易依賴度仍高，日圓任何短期內的急速升值，
都極易觸發全球風險資產的連鎖去槓桿效應。

資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.8

中國8月通膨回升，惟需求端疲弱
• 8月份 CPI及PPI：消費者物 價指 數 (CPI)月增 0.4%(前月月減 0.1%)、年增 0.8%(前月 0.5%)，生 產者物價指數 (PPI) 月增
0.4%(前月月減0.7%)、年增3.8%(前月3.5%)。通膨走揚主要受到能源及通訊設備成本上升影響。
• 觀點：中國自2023年以來，內需市場面臨房地產崩盤 、青年失業率飆升以及公共債務攀升等困境 。今年年初以來，通膨雖
然由負轉正，不過從細項得知，因國際油價飆升、AI硬體設備面臨漲價壓力，本波通膨回升主要是受到供給端影響。外部成
本壓力恐進一步壓抑中國內需消費，中國要在真正意義上走出通縮環境可能還需要更多官方政策發力。彭博預估，在需求未
出現明顯改善的情境下，中國央行需要近一步放寬貨幣政策，預測年底前可能降息10個基點。

資料來源：Bloomberg，截至2026.9.9。

AI產業獲利亮眼，但需留意變現能力和資本效率
• AI獲利增速領先大盤，銷售成長成為獲利關鍵：AI板塊在2026年第一與第二季分別實現52.8%與59.7%的高獲利成長，受高
基期影響，未來四季度平均獲利成長預計放緩至 34.4%，但仍大幅優於非 AI板塊12.3%。預計2026年第三季至2027年第二
季，AI平均銷售成長率將達 27.2%，為非AI成分股(5%)5倍以上 。隨著未來利潤率擴張放緩 (預計未來 6個季度平均僅提升
1.66%，低於過去12個季度的3.52%)，持續的銷售成長是維持獲利的關鍵。
• AI供應鏈內部發展並不均衡： 半導體最為強勁，2026Q3至2027Q2預期銷售成長65.8%，獲利大增112.1%且淨利潤率大幅
擴張8.86%，為產業鏈核心引擎。雲端巨頭表現穩健，營收與獲利增速維持約26%，基礎設施軟體與硬體也有15%~20%營

收與獲利成長 。但應用端軟體成長相對乏力 ，未來四季度獲利預期增速僅 4.3%與6.5%，表現低於標普500非AI股平均水準。
• 資本強度創新高 ，但自由現金流承壓： AI資本支出佔營收比重自 2024年Q1的8.9%一路攀升至 2026年Q2的20.1%，預計
2026年下半年將達到23%的高點。資本支出激增導致自由現金流與資本支出之比(FCF/Capex)從2.48倍滑落至0.69倍，自由
現金流利潤率亦從21%降至13.9%。隨著現金逐漸消耗，企業可能需透過債券市場進行發債融資 ，以維持龐大基礎設施投資。
• 小結與投資建議：AI板塊依然是美股獲利驅動力，但隨利潤率提升空間受限、自由現金流受資本支出壓制，投資邏輯已從全
面暴漲轉向注重變現能力與資本效率。投資建議聚焦上游硬體與半導體，建議持續優選具備高定價能力與實質訂單支撐的半
導體與雲端基礎設施龍頭，留意資本支出佔比過高且自由現金流急劇惡化公司，優先選擇現金流充沛、融資成本低企業。
標普500指數盈利成長：AI vs. 非AI
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.7

標普500指數剔除AI後利潤率或下降
AI股票：自由現金流 vs. 資本支出

歐央9月升息一碼，措辭更鷹經濟預測上修，12月或再升息
• 歐洲央行9月升息一碼：歐央將三大政策利率各上調25個基點，存款利率升至
2.5%，反映能源價格受地緣政治因素推升，同時上調2027年和2028年的核心

半年內需表現更為穩健 ，此外，年末成長動能的增強為 2027年創造了更有利
的延續效應，也推動了明年經濟成長預期的上調。
• 投資建議： 此次會後 聲明基調偏鷹 ，強調通膨衝擊預計還將延續 ，市場預期
12月或再升息 ，這將有助銀行業利差 ，進一步支撐營收與獲利表現 ，同時，
企業投資與融資需求回升，加上製造業景氣改善帶動放款成長，均有助於歐洲

銀行業擴大收益來源， 建議投資人可以適度配置，增加投組多元性。

• 美國8月通膨數據：8月美國CPI月增0.4%、年增3.4%，核心CPI月增0.3%，核心服務通膨升溫至0.5%，顯示通膨降溫仍不均衡，

1碼的壓力升高。不過，本次通膨主要來自能源、需求及AI投資帶動，與2021～2022年供給型通膨有所不同。
• 市場反應與展望：CPI公布後，2年期美債殖利率一度上升，惟長天期殖利率反而走低，顯示市場關注焦點並非單純升息，而是

Fed能否維持抗通膨政策可信度。整體而言，升息未必等同Risk-off，若經濟與企業獲利維持韌性，股市仍具支撐。

前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 本資料所載內容如涉及商品資訊 ，商品相關資訊仍以經核可之條款及文宣 (DM)為主，本行網站已備有公開說明書可供查閱基金應負擔之費用及相關投資風險 。投
資人申購基金係持有基金受益憑證，而非本資料提及之投資資產或標的。
5. 本資料所載內容取自本公司相信可靠之資料來源，且為特定日期之判斷，本公司不保證其即時性、精確性及完整性，亦不提供或嘗試遊說客戶做為投資之判斷依據。

資料內容邇後若有變更，本公司將不做預告或主動更新。投資人於決策時應審慎衡量本身風險，如因信賴本資料內容而做出或改變投資決策，須自行承擔結果，本
公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重 。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。

## Document 170: 8月经济增长数据解读：8月经济运行平稳向新向优

### Page 1

市场研究部 2026年9月15日
事件

## Document 188: A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader (2026-09-19)

Source PDF: all report pdf/A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader (2026-09-19).pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#australia #luciellis #interestrates #businessconditions #credit
A changing investment narrative requires a careful reader
By Luci Ellis
Chief Economist Westpac Group, Westpac
10:43 September 18 2026 Share
Business investment is now in the driver’s seat on growth, implying credit growth is a less
reliable guide to financial conditions more broadly.
Strong business investment, autonomous from government or central bank policy, has filled the gap
left by slower growth in public demand, and changed the narrative around the growth cycle. This has

implications for how we should think about financial conditions, and specifically business credit

### Page 2

Historically, much of the commentary on the growth cycle in Australia has been policy-driven. The RBA
sets the cash rate, which affects housing and mortgage markets, and so consumer spending. Business
investment was seen as largely downstream of a consumer-driven domestic demand cycle, on the logic
that businesses will not invest if demand is not there.

There were exceptions to this, most notably the rise of China and associated mining investment boom.
The pervasive effects of this boom and its unwind were systematically underestimated. Also not fully
appreciated at the time was the deadening effect of risk aversion and weak investment appetite in the
wake of the GFC. But these factors mostly played out over a longer horizon, and the shorter-run rates–
consumer nexus tended to be more in focus.

Another policy-driven narrative became more prominent in recent years, with public demand as the
swing variable. Government infrastructure projects and the growth in the care economy were driving
growth in domestic demand. Initially we worried that when the infrastructure projects ended and the
expansion in the care economy matured, the handover would be ‘shaky’ and growth momentum would
weaken further.

Fortunately, that did not happen. Developments in the private sector, autonomous from domestic policy,
have recently become a key driver and filled the gap as public sector demand growth slowed. In many
respects, the data centre boom and associated renewables spend is rewriting the narrative, and our
recent forecast revisions reflect this.

This shift has several implications for how we should think about financial conditions and specifically
how business credit growth fits into any assessment of financial conditions.

First, business investment is not that correlated with short-term interest rates. This was true in
the post-GFC period of slow growth, when hurdle rates stayed high even though interest rates were
unusually low. It will also be true in the upswing. The hyperscalers and other funders that are driving
the data centre boom are essentially indifferent to cost. They do not seem to care that they are funding
themselves at much wider spreads than similarly rated non-tech firms. Nor do they seem to care about
cost to build. They just want to get it done as fast as possible. But if their activities are impervious to
growth.
Typically, business investment is only loosely correlated with interest rates, and business credit
growth is in turn only loosely correlated with new investment. Business balance sheets strengthened
in the wake of the pandemic, so there might be scope for further increases in leverage. The pre-
pandemic period of low growth, inflation and interest rates is in any case not the relevant period of
comparison for where we are now.
If something is essentially impervious to interest rates, then it cannot be an indicator of financial
conditions more broadly. There is a difference between a claim that policy is restrictive but needs to
be even tighter because of a positive shock, and a claim that policy is not tight at all. These are
important considerations for how we should interpret developments in business credit.

### Page 3

interest rates and spreads, they cannot guide views on how the economy is responding to interest
rates.

Second, business investment is only loosely correlated with business credit quarter to quarter or
year to year. A stylised description of many businesses’ strategies is to use debt funding to acquire
assets or other companies, but use retained earnings to fund new investment. Short-run fluctuations in
business credit growth might therefore provide weak or misleading signals about investment or activity
more broadly. Moreover, if businesses’ funding mix changes, even medium-term shifts in business
credit growth might have little implication for underlying activity.

Third, balance sheets matter. Businesses came out of COVID cashed up, in part because of the fiscal
support they received during the pandemic. Business leverage – whether measured as debt relative to
financial assets broadly or debt to cash plus deposits – dipped below even the low post-GFC average.
It has since returned to that average but might have scope to increase further. This would not
necessarily mean credit supply has eased. Rather, the population of borrowers has effectively changed
and become stronger.

Fourth, and related to the previous point, the comparison period matters. We must recall that the
period between the GFC and the pandemic was the anomaly – low growth, low inflation, low
investment – and extremely low interest rates. The global structure of interest rates is likely to be higher
now and into the future, because of the AI boom, the US fiscal situation and the end of the post-GFC
inflation undershoot. But this is not news, and it is not necessarily a sign of a change in financial
conditions more recently.

This point is particularly salient for total credit growth as an indicator of financial conditions. Even a
return to inflation around or above target, rather than consistently below as in the 2010s, means that
average nominal credit growth rates from that period are not representative of “normal”. Nominal
growth in the economy was slower, and nominal credit growth is downstream from that. Moreover,
many of the factors that made the post-GFC period unusual – the weak risk appetite, regulatory
adjustments and balance sheet repair – would be expected to weigh on credit growth especially
heavily. There is also a cyclical element here: more (real) credit growth might not be saying anything
about credit supply, and so “financial conditions”. Rather, stronger growth makes for stronger borrowers
that are more willing to demand credit and more qualified to access it.

All of this suggests that observers should be cautious in how they interpret business credit conditions.
If something is essentially impervious to interest rates, then it cannot be an indicator of
financial conditions more broadly. To be fair, the hyperscalers are issuing bonds, not borrowing from
the domestic banking system and being recorded in business credit. To the extent that current business
investment and credit growth are being driven by the data centre boom’s broader spillovers to other
sectors, though, that provides almost no signal about the tightness of monetary policy.

### Page 4

Related articles
This is important given the apparent tendency of some recent RBA and other commentary to conflate a
shock with a reason to revise the neutral rate higher. For example, in one recent speech, it was argued
that, because AI was a smaller shock for Australia than some other countries (debateable), long yields
would rise by less than elsewhere. This in turn would result in a lower exchange rate than otherwise,
necessitating higher interest rates. But the exchange rate depreciation is part of the transmission of
that differential response to different-sized shocks. It is not an independent outcome requiring that
interest rates need to be higher, actually. In the face of a positive shock, yes, policy rates would need to
be higher than if the shock had not occurred. But being a lesser beneficiary of that shock is not a
heads-I-hike-tails-your-rates-go-up double-whammy.

This framing is even more curious, when one considers that the RBA never took any signal from the
USD selloff earlier in the year. That selloff had nothing to do with conditions in Australia, but it surely
lifted the AUD exchange rate at the margin – at least against some trading partners – and therefore
was slightly disinflationary.

The broader lesson here is not that this time is different, but rather “last time” – the period between the
GFC and the pandemic – was different. It has also taken a while to normalise from the pandemic itself,
especially where balance sheets and financial behaviour are concerned. If we fail to understand this
recovery away from the post-GFC era, or the implications of more fleeting shocks for financial
behaviour, we risk misconstruing where the economy might be headed and where policy settings need
to be to achieve desired outcomes. The strength of business credit does not have the same
implications as similarly strong household credit would.

The boom in data centres and renewables is reshaping Australia’s economic narrative and needs to be
a consideration in policy settings. But care must be taken to avoid overstating its implications for
financial conditions. There are many good reasons to think that the long-run average for the global
structure of interest rates (and so neutral rates) will be higher in future than pre-pandemic. Current
strong growth in business credit is not necessarily one of them.

## Document 199: AI数据中心又带火一项“新清洁能源”！Meta、谷歌押注长时储能，最长可存100小时

### Page 1

AI 数据中⼼的电⼒需求正加速⻓时储能商业化。⾃ 2025 年初以来，美国新增⻓时储能项⽬
规模已超 150 吉⽡时，较 2024 年底规划总量增⻓逾五倍。 Meta 、⾕歌等科技巨头纷纷布
局，储能时⻓最⻓达 100 ⼩时。⼤规模订单也为初创企业提供市场验证和融资⽀持，推动技
术降本并向更多⾏业扩散。
摘要：
⼈⼯智能数据中⼼的电⼒需求，正在把⼀个沉寂多年的清洁能源赛道重新推向聚光灯下。
Meta 、⾕歌等科技巨头正加⼤对⻓时储能的投⼊，通过⼤规模采购和项⽬合作，为此前成本
⾼、商业化缓慢的新型储能技术打开市场。
9 ⽉ 18 ⽇，据彭博报道，⾃ 2025 年初以来，美国已有⾄少 150 吉⽡时的⻓时储能项⽬宣布、获批
或完成融资，较 2024 年底全国规划装机总量增⻓逾五倍，其中⼤部分新增需求来⾃ AI 数据中
⼼。
与传统锂离⼦电池通常只能提供数⼩时储能不同，⻓时储能可以将电⼒储存数⼗⼩时乃⾄数
天，更适合应对 AI 数据中⼼不断增⻓的⽤电需求，也能够帮助⻛电、光伏等可再⽣能源跨越发

电与⽤电之间的时间错配。
科技巨头的介⼊还带来了此前较为稀缺的商业化验证。⾕歌正与意⼤利公司 Energy Dome 合
作，在亚利桑那州部署可储能 10 ⼩时的⼆氧化碳储能系统； Meta 则与加州企业 Noon Energy 签
署协议，计划安装最⻓可储能 100 ⼩时的系统。随着订单增加，⻓时储能初创企业也有望获得更
多融资并扩⼤产能。
AI 需求激活⻓时储能，技术路线加速落地
⻓时储能涵盖多种技术路线，可持续储存并释放电⼒数⼩时乃⾄数天。过去，这⼀领域⻓期受
制于成本较⾼、缺乏规模化验证，商业化进展缓慢。如今， AI 基础设施扩张正在加速这⼀赛道落
地。 Crusoe 联合创始⼈兼总裁 Cully Cavness 表⽰，⻓时储能正成为扩⼤ AI 基础设施的重要⼯
具。
科技巨头押注的技术路线各不相同：⾕歌与 Energy Dome 合作，利⽤⼆氧化碳实现 10 ⼩时储
能； Crusoe 与 Energy Vault 合作，通过重⼒势能储存电⼒； Meta 与 Noon Energy 的⽅案则可将

储能时⻓延⻓⾄ 100 ⼩时。
Meta 清洁与可再⽣能源负责⼈ Amanda Yang 表⽰， “ 四⼩时的储能能⼒远远不够 ” 。⾕歌先进能
源技术总监 Lucia Tian 则表⽰，部分⻓时储能技术⽆需依赖⽯墨等关键矿物，有助于降低对相关
供应链的依赖。
科技巨头订单为初创企业打开融资窗⼝
对于⻓时储能初创企业⽽⾔，⼤型数据中⼼客⼾的意义不仅在于采购规模，还在于帮助新技术
获得市场认可。
Noon Energy 执⾏副总裁 Aric Saunders 表⽰，⽬前 AI 数据中⼼约占该公司计划部署量的⼀半，
⽽两年前这⼀⽐例⼏乎为零。他称，其他客⼾虽然也在增加，但很少有企业能匹敌 AI 数据中⼼
的采购速度和资⾦实⼒， “ 它们是这个⾏业的加速剂 ” 。

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Commodities: From Inflation Hedge to Structural Growth Asset
Anna Snider, Managing Director and Head of Investment Manager Selection
(Due Diligence)
A broader role in portfolios: For decades, commodities occupied a relatively narrow
role in portfolios: protection against inflation when rising prices eroded the value
of traditional assets. That function remains important, but the investment case is

broadening. Commodities now sit at the intersection of geopolitical fragmentation,
supply-chain security, reshoring, inventory rebuilding, and the buildout of AI and digital
infrastructure. Together, these forces are turning parts of the asset class from purely
cyclical exposures into potential long-term growth beneficiaries.
The diversification case is also becoming more relevant. In Q1, when inflation concerns
peaked, the Bloomberg Commodity Index returned close to 25%, outperforming both

Equities and Fixed Income. At the same time, the relationship among major asset
classes has shifted. Equity and Fixed Income correlations have tightened in recent years,
while commodities have moved in the opposite direction: over the trailing five years,
their correlation with both Equities and bonds has been negative. That combination of
inflation sensitivity and differentiated return behavior can make commodities useful
beyond a simple tactical hedge.

AI infrastructure is a physical buildout: AI is often viewed through semiconductors,
software platforms, and hyperscale data centers. Yet the infrastructure required to
deploy AI is extraordinarily commodity intensive. It requires continuous electricity,
generation capacity, transmission lines, substations, transformers, cooling systems,
networking hardware and structural materials. In that sense, AI is not only a computing
revolution. It is also an energy, infrastructure, and critical-materials investment cycle.

Power is the first constraint. Data centers require reliable, around-the-clock electricity.
Natural gas remains a practical source of incremental generation in the U.S., while
nuclear power is emerging as a strategic long-term option for hyperscalers seeking
stable baseload supply. Solar and wind can add capacity and support decarbonization
goals, but grid reliability and storage become more important as power demand rises.
That supports investment in batteries, backup power and grid-scale storage, with lithium

and graphite among the relevant inputs.
The buildout also increases demand for industrial metals. Copper is a clear beneficiary
because it is essential to transmission, transformers, cooling systems and data-center
wiring. Aluminum and iron ore are used in facilities, electrical equipment, transmission
networks and supporting infrastructure. Semiconductor fabrication adds another layer
of demand through industrial gases such as helium and neon, high-purity quartz for

wafers and optics, and gallium and germanium for advanced chips, photonics, and
telecommunications. AI-enabled automation and robotics also depend on permanent
magnets that use rare earth elements, including neodymium-praseodymium and
dysprosium-terbium.
Demand has moved faster than supply: This demand surge resembles earlier cycles
in semiconductors and memory, where expectations pulled spending forward. Metals
and mining companies are already seeing earnings growth and margin expansion,

yet the industry has struggled to keep pace with demand. The imbalance matters
because commodity supply is slow to respond. Production of many strategic materials
is geographically concentrated, permitting timelines are lengthy, recycling capacity is
limited, and substitution can remain years from commercial viability. New mines and
processing facilities can take a decade or more to reach production.
Investment Implications
Commodities may increasingly serve

three portfolio purposes: inflation
protection, diversification from
traditional assets, and participation in
long-term infrastructure and resource-
demand growth, supporting a diversified
allocation across direct commodities
and select producer Equities rather than
a single directional trade.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Governments are also taking a larger role through industrial policy, export controls,
stockpiling and efforts to secure critical supply chains. Restrictions involving gallium,
germanium, graphite and other strategic materials underscore how geopolitics can affect
availability and pricing. As a result, the current cycle may produce greater scarcity and
pricing power than demand growth alone would suggest.

So far, this dynamic has been most visible in industrial metals, but the same forces could
extend into agriculture. Reshoring changes where food and inputs are produced and
processed, while inventory rebuilding can lift demand for fertilizer, fuel, machinery and
storage. Agriculture has not yet experienced the same earnings and pricing impact as
metals, but supply-chain resilience could make it a broader beneficiary over time.
Cyclical forces still matter: The structural thesis does not eliminate near-term

volatility. Energy remains a key transmission mechanism for inflation because oil and
natural gas underpin transportation, manufacturing and industrial production. Geopolitical
disruptions can cascade through supply chains, raising costs and moving both commodity
futures and spot prices. Currency dynamics matter as well. A stronger U.S. dollar can
restrain commodity prices by increasing costs for non-U.S. buyers, while renewed dollar
weakness could support emerging-market demand and reprice dollar-denominated

assets.
A stabilization of geopolitical tensions could create a more constructive cyclical backdrop
through lower energy volatility, improved business confidence and stronger capital
investment. Conversely, renewed conflict can produce sharp and uneven moves across
the complex. These crosscurrents reinforce the value of treating commodities as a
diversified allocation rather than a single directional trade.
Gold, implementation and the portfolio case: Gold is fundamentally different from

most industrial commodities, but it deserves attention. Its traditional relationships with
interest rates and the dollar have been less reliable lately. Even so, sustained central bank
and exchange-traded fund buying points to durable long-term demand despite bouts of
volatility such as the selloff earlier this year. Gold also tended to move inversely with oil
during the height of the Iran conflict, illustrating how different commodities can respond

differently to the same geopolitical shock.
Investors can access the theme through commodity futures or producer Equities. Direct
commodity exposure is generally the clearer vehicle for inflation protection, scarcity, and
geopolitical supply shocks. Producer equities can offer greater upside when commodity
prices rise faster than operating costs because earnings and margins expand, but that
leverage is asymmetric. Company-specific execution, financing, cost and valuation risks

can still cause Equities to underperform the underlying commodity. A blended approach
may capture both physical scarcity and corporate operating leverage.
Ultimately, commodities remain sensitive to energy markets, currencies, growth, and
geopolitics, but their portfolio role is expanding. They can provide inflation protection and
diversification while offering exposure to AI infrastructure, electrification, energy security,
defense modernization, reshoring and, potentially, agricultural supply-chain investment.

The strongest beneficiaries of the AI trade may therefore extend well beyond technology
companies to the materials and power systems that make deployment possible.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Rising Global Interest Rates and International Markets
Ehiwario Efeyini, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Strong earnings growth has so far delivered a fourth consecutive year of double-digit
gains for global Equity indexes in 2026, propelling them to new all-time highs. But a
growing risk throughout the year has been the increase in government bond yields, which

have also broken out to multi-decade highs across a number of the major markets. The
U.S. Treasury will this week begin its expanded Treasury securities buyback program
announced in mid-August in response to rising rates. But 10-year yields across all
the major G10 countries have moved higher, in many cases by as much as or more
than in the U.S. market. Against a backdrop of above-trend growth in nominal gross
domestic product (GDP), high energy prices and elevated inflation, investors appear to

be reassessing monetary policy expectations, particularly in Europe where most of the
2026 increase in 10-year yields has been driven by inflation breakevens (Exhibit 1). Fixed
Income markets are consequently looking for policy rate hikes from the European Central
Bank later this week and potentially from the Bank of Japan later next month.
The rise in global interest rates has also come alongside an expansion in fiscal policy
across the major markets. In Europe, 2026 and 2027 are expected to be the peak

spending years for defense and infrastructure expenditure plans unveiled last year. The
so-called Readiness 2030 package allocates €800 billion to increased European Union
defense spending as it looks to move toward its 5% of GDP target by 2035. And at the
same time, the German government relaxation of its national debt brake has directed
€500 billion toward funding for investment in clean energy, transportation and digital
infrastructure. Similarly in Japan, the government supermajority is looking to enact a

new stimulus aimed at raising investment levels across 17 strategic sectors such as
semiconductors, defense and shipbuilding, in addition to a planned two-year cut to the
consumption tax on food from April 2027. And in the UK, the new central government
leadership is also likely to announce new spending measures when its first national
budget is released next month. To the extent that increased public investment spending
can lift productivity and support nominal GDP growth in Europe and Japan, the rise in

yields need not undermine economic growth or derail the equity market advance. But
higher rates, especially in response to supply-driven energy price inflation, nonetheless
remain a risk for developed market equity returns going into the end of the year.
Exhibit 1: Global Bond Yields in Major Markets have Risen by as Much or More
than U.S. Yields in 2026.

For many emerging markets the picture looks quite different. In contrast with the U.S.,
Japan and Europe, inflation rates are generally well below central bank targets outside
a limited number of resource exporters in Latin America and the booming AI-linked
economies of Korea and Taiwan (Exhibit 2). As a result, policy rate expectations over

the coming quarters are stable or lower for several emerging markets (including China,
Malaysia, Thailand, Poland, Brazil and Mexico) according to Bloomberg consensus forecasts.
Investment Implications
Ongoing strength in economic growth
and corporate earnings should support
global equity markets even in the face
of rising rates. For Emerging Markets
(EM) Equities in particular, contained
inflation, flat to lower local policy rate
expectations and stronger fundamentals

should limit the impact. And we
continue to favor EM Equities over
International Developed Markets, where
inflation levels are generally still above
target, central banks are expected
to raise rates and investors will be
watching for fiscal sustainability as
governments implement expansionary
budget plans.

Historically, rising U.S. interest rates have been a major headwind for emerging markets.
But looking ahead in the current cycle, we still expect the impact of potential Federal
Reserve (Fed) rate hikes to be lower than in the past. Over recent years, the association
between tighter Fed policy and weaker EM returns appears to have diminished. EM
Equities for example were resilient in the face of aggressive U.S. rate increases in 2022
and 2023, broadly keeping pace with the rest of the world. This is in sharp contrast to

prior cycles in which tighter Fed policy has resulted in capital flight, deleveraging and
falling asset values across the emerging world. Particularly during the crisis periods of
the 1980s and 1990s—but also more recently during the 2015 taper tantrum—U.S.
monetary tightening has tended to attract capital away from riskier emerging countries
(particularly those with large current account deficits), reducing support for local asset
prices and exchange rates.

Associated strength in the U.S. dollar has typically been an additional hurdle. For those
emerging economies with fixed exchange rates, a loss of export competitiveness has
further undermined current account positions, forcing central banks to run down their
foreign exchange reserves. And where exchange rates are floating, foreign currency
debts have risen in local currency terms, resulting in widespread defaults, debt
downgrades, recessions and additional exchange rate depreciation.

But starting in the years that followed the 1997-1998 Asian financial crisis and
continuing into the current cycle, the fundamentals across much of the EM universe have
improved. Fixed exchange rates have been abandoned. EM foreign currency reserves have
roughly doubled as a share of GDP . The share of EM debt denominated in U.S. dollars
has fallen significantly, led by the private sector across households and corporates. And
for most countries, current account deficits have narrowed or disappeared entirely. As a

result, emerging markets have become much more insulated from the direction of U.S.
interest rates on a structural basis, and this has only been reinforced by the AI-driven
revaluation of key Asian markets in 2026. Greater concentration in Korea and Taiwan has
increased the EM weighting in the surplus Asia-Pacific region to 83% as at the end of
August—up from 81% at the start of the year and 71% a decade ago.
Above-trend growth in nominal GDP and ongoing strength in corporate earnings should

support global equity markets even in the face of rising interest rates. But for emerging
Equities in particular, contained inflation relative to central bank targets, flat to lower
local policy rate expectations and stronger economic fundamentals should limit the
impact from higher global rates. We continue to favor EM Equities over International
Developed Markets, where inflation levels are generally still above target, central banks
are expected to raise interest rates and investors will be watching for fiscal sustainability

as governments implement expansionary budget plans.

### Page 6

6 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The One-Offs Inflating Q2 Earnings
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Headline Q2 earnings numbers look almost too good to be true. And in a sense, they are.
With nearly all of S&P 500 companies’ results reported, EPS growth is tracking around
50% year-over-year (YoY)—not just an acceleration from Q1 but a second consecutive
quarter above 20%. Chiefly skewing the headline number are massive mark-to-market

gains on private investments for a handful of mega cap technology companies like
Alphabet and Amazon that booked outsized non-operating gains tied to their stakes in
Anthropic, while Microsoft benefited from its OpenAI investment. Strip those out, and
adjusted EPS growth notches down to 31%. The point being, after being sliced and
diced, 30%+ is well above any long-run historical earnings growth rate.
The one-off nature of earnings strength wasn’t limited to investment gains. A second,

less-discussed boost was tariff refunds. As Exhibit 3 illustrates, revenues from tariffs
steadily climbed from early 2025 through mid-2026 until the International Emergency
Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) tariff ruling which initiated the refund process. As
refunds were disbursed, customs receipts turned net negative in June and July, with
refund payments exceeding total tariff collections. Customs and Border Protection cash
withdrawals of more than $100 billion in refunds have now been paid—60% of the $165

billion in total eligible claims.
The recipients: the “importers of record,” or the dozens of S&P 500 companies that
have disclosed billions in refunds over the past two quarters; Apple alone received $2.19
billion, adding $0.11 to per-share earnings to their quarter. Other disclosed refunds
include Nike with $986 million, Amazon with $640 million, and General Motors refunded
$500 million (although accounting treatment and use of proceeds has varied).

The boost of investment gains and/or refunds may not be repeatable in future quarters.
Perhaps more telling is that the median company’s revenue grew roughly 15% YoY, the
strongest gain in years, corroborating that there is operating momentum rather than
just an accounting mirage for Q2 earnings. Margins, too, as a percent of GDP are at
their highest back to 1950, hovering north of 22%, see Exhibit 4. That this is occurring
amid rising rates and at a time of geopolitical uncertainty/disruption, rather than a post-

recessionary recovery, makes the results that much more impressive.
The Path Forward: Questions remain about the sustainability of these elevated growth
rates, although consensus expects EPS growth to stay above 20% in both Q3 and Q4.
If achieved, every quarter this year would see 20%-plus EPS growth. On the back of
brighter outlooks from Corporate America in recent weeks, full-year earnings growth
will decelerate into the mid-teens in 2027, but that moderation comes off a high base

of 2026. For now, the breadth of earnings growth is the encouraging story as 10 of
the 11 sectors reported growth (Energy and Communication Services leading the way,
Healthcare the only detractor). That’s a reflection of strong core businesses growing
revenues into 2027’s tougher YoY comparisons.
Investment Implications
Underlying earnings-per-share (EPS)
growth of around 30%, stripped
of investment gains, is historically
exceptional. It remains that robust

corporate earnings and positive
revisions have helped Equities absorb
concerns around the recent rise
in yields.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.

## Document 252: Fed builds credibility with hike; one more likely in Dec

Source PDF: all report pdf/Fed builds credibility with hike; one more likely in Dec.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Fed builds credibility with 
hike; one more likely in Dec 
Summary 
Event: The US Federal Reserve hiked its key interest rate by 
25bps to 3.75-4.0%, in a unanimous decision. This marks the 
first hike since 2023, reversing the easing cycle that had 
taken rates down from 4.25% in September 2025 to a trough 
of 3.75% by December 2025. The revised projections 
indicate the Fed expects to raise rates by an additional 25bps 
this year, hold rates in 2027, and cut 25bps in both 2028 and 
2029. It upgraded the long-run rate slightly to 3.2%.  
Market reaction: The US yield curve mildly flattened , while 
the S&P500 index fell 0.5%. The policy rate sensitive US 2 -
year government bond yield jumped 7 bps to 4. 74% as 
markets factored in  three more 25bps hikes by June 2027 . 
The US 10-year yield rose 2bps to 5.02%, its highest since 
2007, while the 30-year yield was little changed. The USD 
index (DXY) rose 0.6%, breaking past its 50- and 100-DMAs. 
Investment strategy: Today’s move realises what we had 
been flagging in recent weeks i.e. the rising risk of a Fed rate 
hike following Fed Chair Warsh’s Jackson Hole speech and 
recent economic data prints, namely: a pick-up in job creation 
and inflation over the summer and a sharp rebound in oil 
prices due to the re-escalation of the Middle East conflict.  
This hike helps the Fed rebuild credibility as an inflation 
fighter. Given the unanimous decision to hike rates this week, 
we believe there will likely be one more hike before the end 
of this year, and one more in H1 2027 as we expect robust 
growth amid strong AI investments and a tight labour market, 
keeping inflation elevated.  That scenario would still be less 
than the three more rate hikes money markets are pricing 
after the latest Fed move. We believe policy rates can return 
to the 4.0 –4.25% range by end-2027, with the possibility of 
cuts in H2 2027  as the impact of tariffs and high oil prices 
start to fade towards the second half of 2027.   
Today’s refreshed Fed policy rate path is mildly bullish for the 
USD index; so we revise up our 3 -month target to 100. We 
also revise up our 3-month 10-year yield target to 5.0-5.25%.  
 The USD rose, while stocks fell after the Fed hiked rates 
and signalled one more rate hike this year; ultra-long 
bonds rose slightly as the Fed rebuild its inflation-
fighting credentials 
Asset performance: 15-Sep close to 16-Sep close

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered

The Fed slightly revised up its growth projections for 
2026-27 and inflation projection for 2026; however, it 
still sees inflation cooling over the next two years

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 17 September 2026

Backdrop to the rate hike 
In hiking rates, Fed Chair Warsh reaffirmed the inflation 
threat to the US economy. US headline inflation accelerated 
to 0.4% m/m in August and stayed elevated on an annualised 
basis at 3.4% y/y. Energy prices , driven by the Middle East 
conflict, were a primary contributor. Oil prices have extended 
gains since the August inflation report, surging past USD 
100/bbl. Meanwhile, core inflation rose 0.3% m/m in August, 
beating estimates for a 0.2% rise . Also, US producer prices 
jumped 0.4% m/m in August, the most since May, and 5.4%  
y/y, further reinforcing the inflationary backdrop. 
The inflation report follow ed a stronger -than-expected jobs 
report for August. US monthly job creation has surged to an 
average 80,000 this year, from 10,000, tightening a job 
market that already faces a supply crunch from the Trump 
administration’s clampdown against immigration.  The 
combination of an acceleration in job creation and inflation 
over the summer, combined with a rebound in oil prices, 
supported our expectation for a Fed rate hike this week.  
Removing “a dose of accommodation” 
Warsh framed the decision to hike as removing "a dose of 
accommodation", rather than the start of an aggressive rate 
hiking cycle, and we agree with that characterisation. The aim 
is to bring financial and credit conditions more in line with the 
Fed's price stability objectives. To quote Warsh: “Our 
predominant focus is on the price stability side of our 
mandate. The plain fact is that inflation is too high, and has 
been for too long….This  summer’s inflation readings do not 
tell me that underlying trends have meaningfully improved.” 
He added that many categories in the last consumer and 
producer inflation reports are still showing price increases 
above 3% on a six- and 12-month basis.  
The unanimous vote to hike rates, contrasts with a 9 -3 split 
vote in July to hold rates. When combined with the pencilling 
in of one more hike by December, this suggests a majority of 
Fed policymakers have turned more hawkish over the 
summer. In doing so, they have helped rebuild the Fed’s 
inflation-fighting credentials and its independence against 
President Trump’s pressures to cut rates. This explains why 
long-end bond yields were stable after this week’s hike. 
Nevertheless, Warsh provided little guidance on the future 
rate path, as he held what was reportedly the shortest post -
meeting press conference on record for a Fed chair . This is 
consistent with his stated preference for less central bank 
communication. We expect more clarity once the Fed task 
forces submit their reports by year end. 
— Jonathan Liang, CIO for Fixed Income and FX   
Rajat Bhattacharya, Senior Investment Strategist

## Document 278: Likely case Apple is stepping into enterprise AI server market_

### Page 1

From:    陸⾏之  Andrew Lu on global semis and techs <andrewhclu@substack.com>
Subject: Likely case Apple is stepping into enterprise AI server market?
Date:    1 day ago
View in browser
Likely case Apple is stepping
into enterprise AI server
market?
蘋果⽤⾃研晶⽚配合  Nvidia NVLink Fusion 技術打算加⼊企業⽤ AI
伺服器市場
ANDREW LU ON GLOBAL SEMI/TECHS
SEP 16 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP
Reuters 引述  The Information 新聞報導（Apple Considers Return to
Server Market, Has Talked With Nvidia to Use Network Tech）， Apple

正研究重新進⼊企業⽤伺服器市場，以  2 或  4 顆  M8 Ultra 晶⽚建構  AI 推理
伺服器，並考慮採⽤  NVIDIA NVLink Fusion 連接  M8 chips 。產品最早約
2029 ，⽬前仍可能取消或最終不採  NVIDIA ； Reuters 無法獨⽴驗證。 Apple
⾃ 2002 年發表 Xserve 及 2011 年停⽌  Xserve 產品後，若此案落地將是  18
年後重新出售  dedicated server hardware 。
What’s new?

### Page 2

Source: The Information
NVIDIA ： 最重要。 NVLink Fusion 的戰略價值是即使  AI 運算單元
compute die 不是  NVIDIA GPU ， NVIDIA 仍可供應  scale-up
connectivity 的技術。 Apple 是極端垂直整合的公司，若連  Apple 都認為
NVIDIA NVLink Fusion 是最佳晶⽚網路連結選項， NVLink 的平台護城河很
⾼。
付費訂閱戶可分享我們對於受惠及負⾯廠商分析，給投
資⼈的建議及  Outside the box ⾮主流想法
Subscribed
Apple：  M-series silicon 可從消費性及專業⼯作站電腦延伸到企業⽤ AI 推
理伺服器，增加蘋果晶⽚的可接觸市場  TAM ，新產品營收增量動能，與攤銷
龐⼤的設計成本及拉⾼獲利率。
TSMC：  Apple 伺服器晶⽚及 Nvidia NVLink Fusion 將增加台積電先進製
程的需求，與⼤型 CoWoS 封測，⾼速  I/O 晶⽚需求。

Optics / networking ecosystem：  2→4 顆晶⽚組合只是起點；若未來
擴到  rack-scale ， switch 、 retimer 、 AEC/optics 與  high-speed
connectors content 會上升。
Positive Impacts to supply chain vendors

### Page 3

傳統  x86 inference server CPU vendors：若  Apple 的
performance/W 速度及耗能優勢能移植到伺服器，部分  private/enterprise
inference workload 可能被⾃研 CPU 晶⽚吸收，這對 x86 伺服器 CPU 供應商
Intel/AMD 不利。
純  GPU-share bearish thesis 也會受挑戰：⾃研  ASIC/CPU 份額增加並
不必然使  NVIDIA 營收下降，因  NVIDIA 可以從  NVLink Fusion 技術收費，
但 AMD AI GPU 的 TAM 就會受到影響。
Apple ⾃⼰的⾵險則是  enterprise software/support ecosystem：
硬體性能不是伺服器市場唯⼀⾨檻； MLX 、 framework support 、 ﬂeet
management 、 service/SLA 都需建⽴。
短期：  這是  2029 年才會量產的產品，不應計⼊  2027–28 EPS 。真正可交易

的是  NVIDIA NVLink Fusion 的⽣態系擴充的估值是否有提升的空間。
2029 年後：  應分別觀察  NVIDIA 的  AI GPU + Scale-up fabric + Scale-
out networking + CUDA Software 四個核⼼競爭⼒，⽽⾮只估  AI GPU 的
出貨量。
已知事實：  雖尚未定案， Apple 正考慮⾃研  M8 Ultra AI server + NVIDIA
NVLink Fusion ，對於⾃研 ASIC 晶⽚屬於第⼀梯隊的蘋果⽽⾔，⾸次從企業
⽤ AI 推理伺服器切⼊市場，我們覺得⽽且⾮常合理；
我們的推論：  NVIDIA 最理想的終局不⼀定是阻⽌  ASIC/TPU 市場做⼤，⽽是
讓所有⾃研  ASIC/TPU 都必須經過  NVIDIA fabric 。
也就是從： Intel era ： CPU owns platform → NVIDIA GPU era ：
accelerator owns platform → AI heterogeneous era ： fabric owns

platform 。
如果  Apple 、 MediaTek ASIC 、 Meta MTIA 、其他  hyperscaler XPU 都能
透過  NVLink Fusion 接⼊  NVIDIA 系統， NVIDIA 可能從「晶⽚市占」轉為
AI interconnect toll road 。這會使  GPU 出貨量市場份額衰退與  NVIDIA ⽑
利市場份額變化脫鉤。
反向情境是  UALink / Ethernet scale-up 或  hyperscaler proprietary
fabrics 成功標準化， NVLink 無法成為跨  vendor default ；那麼  NVIDIA
fabric premium 將被壓縮。
Who can be hurt the most?

General:
All aggregates are bottom-up aggregates calculated from analysts' consensus forecasts unless otherwise noted. Bottom-up
aggregates are weighted calculations using the I/B/E/S data from LSEG share class monitored or the official index weight of
the index-provider. Top-down forecasts are compilations of strategists' estimates. I/B/E/S universe calculations are based on

all companies within a given market monitored by LSEG.
Benchmark index calculations, including those for official local market indices, the S&P 500 Index, and the FTSE World Equity
Indices are calculated using official index constituents and weightings. All index composites are representative of current
index constituents. Index constituents and weightings are updated monthly during options expiration (the Thursday before the
third Friday of every month) for every index except the S&P 500, the constituents of which are current as of the date of

calculation.
PEIGBY refers to the Price Earnings Inverse Government Bond Yield ratio, which measures equity valuation with respect to
the inverse-yield on long-term government bonds (now known as the 'Fed' model). All PEIGBY ratios are I/B/E/S universe-
specific except for the US ratio, where it is based on the S&P 500 Index.
Methodology:
For US and selected Canadian aggregates, calendar-year aggregation is performed using the sum of four quarters' estimates

and a quarterly pro-ration is applied to non-December year end companies' quarterly EPS estimates. Outside of the US and
Canada, all company-level data are calendarized prior to aggregation using the Compustat method of calendarization.
Aggregation is performed using the annual consensus EPS estimate and a May roll-back is applied to December year-end
markets. Aggregates for Japan, where most companies report in March, use an August rollback and a November rollback is

used for Australia and New Zealand, where the fiscal year-end for most companies is in June.
All 12-month-forward data for ex-US markets are calculated using a pro-rata weighting to annual EPS forecasts. The
weightings applied are a daily calibration out of 365 days relative to each constituent's individual fiscal year-end and the
current date.
All non-surprise data, including aggregate forecast earnings, forecast growth, and forward-looking valuation calculations are

weighted according to the I/B/E/S share class monitored for I/B/E/S universes or the weightings assigned to each constituent
in a given index. Aggregated earnings calculations are the equivalent of net profit aggregates, as they are derived from the
EPS forecasts aggregated to the net income level by the appropriate share weighting.
Earnings Aggregation:
For 12-month-forward earnings, the 12-month-forward EPS is used in place of the calendarized EPS in the formulas below.

Growth Calculations:
All growth calculations are the weighted year over year growth in earnings for the calendarized fiscal year period. The
formulas below use index shares for index growth calculations.
Calendarized Earnings Growth = 12-month-forward Growth =
Valuation:
All valuation calculations are the weighted-average price-earnings ratio for the fiscal period in question. The formulas below

use index shares for index P/E calculations.
Calendarized P/E Aggregate = 12-month-forward P/E =
Revisions Momentum:
Unless otherwise indicated, analysts' revisions momentum is a simple aggregation of the total number of estimates raised
divided by the total number of estimates lowered. Thus, any revisions ratio less than 1 indicates negative revisions
momentum while a ratio greater than 1 indicates positive revisions momentum, with 1 marking parity.

The Global Aggregates from LSEG product contains annual aggregates for universes included in this publication, including
supplemental aggregate data of over 100 items as well as history dating back to 1985 for North America and to 1987 for other
markets. To learn more about this product, contact your LSEG sales representative.

## Document 291: OPEC有志７ヵ国､自主減産終了後の10月は様子見姿勢で合意 ～OPECプラスの結束にほころび､生産政策の不透明感高まる懸念も～

Source PDF: all report pdf/OPEC有志７ヵ国､自主減産終了後の10月は様子見姿勢で合意 ～OPECプラスの結束にほころび､生産政策の不透明感高まる懸念も～.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 2
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

World Trends

2026 年 9 月 7 日

1/2 
OPEC 有志７ヵ国､自主減産終了後の 10 月は様子見姿勢で合意 
～OPEC プラスの結束にほころび､生産政策の不透明感高まる懸念も～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ OPEC プラスの有志７ヵ国は９月６日の閣僚会合で、10 月の生産量を据え置くことを決定し
た。有志７ヵ国は、イラン情勢悪化による原油高を受けて４月以降、自主減産を段階的に縮
小し、９月には日量165 万バレル規模の自主減産を解消した。しかし、ホルムズ海峡の事実
上の封鎖やウクライナ戦争の激化により、湾岸産油国やロシア、カザフスタンの生産・輸出
が混乱し、増産合意の多くは実際の供給増につながっていない。７月の有志７ヵ国の産油量
は日量約2,400 万バレルと２月を大幅に下回り、 生産枠にも届かないため、OPEC プラスの原
油価格や市場シェアへの影響力は制約されている。米国とイランの対立激化で原油価格が高
止まりするなか、有志７ヵ国は様子見姿勢を維持した格好である。 
○ 一方で、OPEC プラスは2022 年に合意した日量200 万バレルの協調減産を2026 年末まで維持
する方針だが、2027 年以降の扱いを巡る調整は難航する可能性がある。UAE の OPEC 脱退と増
産に続き、 イラクが生産枠の大幅引き上げを求め、 ベネズエラにも脱退観測が浮上するなど、
枠組みの結束には綻びがみられる。今後は加盟国の生産能力の再評価を巡る利害対立が激し
くなる可能性があり、OPEC プラスは組織の結束維持と原油価格の安定を両立させる難しい舵
取りを迫られるであろう。

【有志７ヵ国は10 月の原油生産方針を据え置き､自主減産終了後は様子見姿勢】 
主要産油国の枠組みである OPEC プラスの有志７ヵ国（サウジアラビア、ロシア、イラク、クウ
ェート、カザフスタン、アルジェリア、オマーン）は、９月６日にオンラインで閣僚会合を開催し
た。イラン情勢の悪化による原油価格の上昇を受けて、 有志国は４月以降、2023 年に合意した日量
165 万バレル規模の自主減産を段階的に縮小させてきた。そして、有志国は８月会合において、９
月に自主減産をすべて解消することで合意した（注１）。 
しかし、同情勢の悪化によりホルムズ海峡が事実上封鎖されるなか、 湾岸産油国は大幅な減産を
強いられている。さらに、ウクライナ戦争も激化するなか、足元ではロシアやカザフスタンの輸出

注１ ８月３日付レポート「OPEC 有志７ヵ国､９月の増産合意で自主減産の解除完了へ」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/638278.html）

### Page 2

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
2/2 
も混乱しており、増産合意の大部分は実際の生産増にはつながっていない。その結果、７月時点に
おける有志７ヵ国の産油量は日量約 2,400 万バレルと２月時点 （同2,931 万バレル）を大きく下回
るうえ、 生産枠に対しても約660 万バレル下回る水準にとどまる。 このため、OPEC プラスによる原
油価格や市場シェアに対する影響力が制限される展開が続いている。 
一方で、足元では米国とイランが船舶
に対する攻撃の応酬を繰り広げるなど、
イラン情勢を巡る不透明感が高まってい
る。これを受けて原油価格は高止まりし
ており （図１） 、 一部の国にとっては財政
均衡水準を上回っている。 こうしたなか、
有志７ヵ国は 10 月の生産量を据え置く
ことで様子見姿勢を 維持 した格好であ
る。 会合後に公表された声明では、10 月
以降の政策に関する言及はなく、引き続
き「協力宣言」の完全な順守の実現に向
けた決意をあらためて表明したとの表現にとどめた。

【OPECプラスの結束にほころび､価格安定との両立に難しさが増す】 
OPEC プラス全体としては、2022 年に合意した日量200 万バレルの協調減産を2026 年末まで維持
する方針を示している。しかし、 今後は期日が近づくなかでその扱いに関する議論が活発化するこ
とが予想される。ＵＡＥ（アラブ首長国連邦）が2026 年５月に OPEC（石油輸出国機構）から脱退
するとともに、 その後は増産の動きを強めていることを受けて、 枠組みの結束に綻びが生じる兆し
がみられる。 
報道では、イラクが OPEC による生産枠の大幅引き上げを要望し、仮に受け入れられなければ脱
退を検討していることが明らかになっている。さらに、８月末にはベネズエラが OPEC からの脱退
を検討し、米国と協議を進めている旨の報道も出ている。背景には、原油価格の下落を狙うトラン
プ米政権が、 ベネズエラとの連携強化を通じてOPEC の影響力の低下を図っているとの見方もある。 
こうしたなか、OPEC プラスとしては、2027 年以降の生産方針を協議する前提として、加盟国の
生産能力の再評価を行う必要がある。 その協議を巡って、 加盟国によるつばぜり合いが激化するこ
とが予想されるとともに、 その行方によっては枠組みの結束力が弱まる可能性も考えられる。OPEC
プラスとしては枠組みの結束と原油価格の安定の間で難しい舵取りを迫られるであろう。

## Document 303: Rates Spark_ Global long-end gets some relief from the BoE

Source PDF: all report pdf/Rates Spark_ Global long-end gets some relief from the BoE.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Article | 17 September 2026 1
THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article  |  17 September 2026   RATES SPARK
Rates Spark: Global long-end gets some
relief from the BoE
Energy concerns eased somewhat, but long-end rates reacted more
strongly to the Bank of England s decision to halt long-end gilt sales. In
Europe, eyes also remain on France as the 2027 budget takes shape
along with upcoming rating reviews
The Bank of England has decided to halt long-end gilt sales, leading to 30Y gilt yields dropping 30bp on

the day
Bank of England getting creative with the very long end
The Bank of England surprised us with a big revamp of their quantitative tightening (QT)
framework, helping 30Y gilt yields to go down 12bp on the day. Headlines first focused on the
Bank’s ambition to bring the entire bond portfolio down to zero over the coming years, which
would add significant supply for the market to absorb. But the changes to the framework are
more nuanced. For one, around £120bn gilts with the longest maturities will remain on the

central bank’s balance sheet, which means bonds maturing after 2049 will not be part of the
active sales.
But perhaps a stronger signal to markets was the decision to pause active sales until April 2027
and explore a mechanism to transfer gilts from the Bank of England to the UK government
treasury. Government-funded QT does not impact the total supply for the market to absorb,

### Page 2

THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article | 17 September 2026 2
since any purchases would have to be financed by new issuance. But it can help transform the
maturity profile, and this is why 30Y gilts are taking a liking to the announcement. Longer-
dated gilts will de facto be replaced by shorter gilt issuance.
Friday’s events and market view
We will get the eurozone current account for July and the European Central Bank will
release results of its inflation survey, where longer-run consumer expectations are

expected to have nudged up on the back of energy prices. ECB President Lagarde will
join the press conference following the ECOFIN meetings.
The US will release industrial production data for August. Fed officials scheduled to
speak are Bowman and Schmid.
Some more attention will fall on France in the evening as DBRS (AA/Stable) and Scope
(AA+/Negative) have scheduled potential rating reviews. Both ratings are higher
compared to the three larger agencies (A+/Aa3/A+), but without a final budget yet, it

might be premature to make any changes just now, especially more than just an
outlook change. That said, PM Lecornu presented plans on Thursday to contain
spending and keep the 2027 deficit at 5%. At 96bp, the 10y OAT/Bund spread remains
at its highest levels since 2012, reflecting unease ahead of next year's presidential
elections.
Other scheduled rating reviews from the three major agencies are for Ireland (S&P
AA+/Stable), Germany (Moody’s Aaa/Stable) and Greece (Moody’s Baa/Stable).

## Document 316: Strategic product vulnerability_ China least, then Europe and then the US (2026-09-16)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Strategic product vulnerability_ China least, then Europe and then the US (2026-09-16).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-16
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

16 September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
SOURCE: INSTITUTE OF INTERNATIONAL FINANCE, LSEG, MACROBONDCHART
STRATEGIC PRODUCT VULNERABILITY? CHINA LEAST, THEN EUROPE AND THEN THE US
Koen De Leus
“Germany is too dependent on the US for its security, on Russia for its energy and on China for its exports.” That was, in essence, Brookings’
Constanze Stelzenmüller’s diagnosis in June 2022, and it was – and is – valid as well for Europe as a whole.

But does this dependency also apply to technological and industrial products? The European Commission’s EXternal Vulnerability Index
(EXVI) answers that very question, mapping out the EU’s exposure to foreign supply chains.
Relatively good results for the EU
The EU is relatively well positioned, with a score of 0.18 on net-zero technologies and 0.22 on semiconductors. Raw materials appear to be the
weakest point (0.28). Interestingly, compared to its peers, the EU is more vulnerable than China across all three chains (0.13), but significantly

less vulnerable than the US overall (0.22 vs. 0.28). The only sector where the EU score is weaker than the US one is on semi-conductors, and
the difference is not large (0.22 vs 0.19). Like the EU, the US’ vulnerability is biggest in raw material (0.32). This is, among other factors, due to
outsourcing polluting mining starting in the 1990s. Looking at the essential components for the green transition, the EU score on raw materials is
concerning, but the better one on net-zero technologies is more encouraging.

The EU challenge is not its dependencies but its relatively weak global position
Taking a closer look at the two pillars across the different products, it becomes clear that Europe’s problem is mainly related to its global position
(Pillar 2). Dependency (Pillar 1) itself is not the core issue, with much lower scores than the ones for Pillar 2. The EU dependency risk on raw mate-
rials is even comparable to China score (0.24 vs 0.23), and the EU score on semi-conductors (0.13) is the lowest of the other results for the region.

On the other hand, Pillar 2 scores are more negative, illustrating the lack of EU competitiveness in these areas, with scores hovering between 0.29
for net-zero technologies up to a high 0.39 for semiconductors and 0.41 for raw materials. Europe simply does not produce enough of these pro -
ducts at competitive prices, as big European companies in these sectors are limited. Take semiconductors, for example. Imports are not extremely
EXVI is built on two pillars, with two simple questions for each pr oduct: 1/ How dependent is a r egion on a handful of for eign suppliers? 2/ How

globally competitive is it in that pr oduct measured by its imports versus e xports? The result is a single score from 0 (low vulnerability) to 1
(high). A region scores high (vulnerable) when ther e is both a heavy  reliance on others and a weak market position.
Strategic product vulnerability? China least, then Europe and then the US

### Page 2

2
La banque
d’un monde
qui change
Chart of the Week / 16 September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
One trend is undeniable: China consistently enjoys the lowest vulnerability. Decades of strategic plans, as well as subsidies and other practices
to restrict open markets, have enabled it to build up domestic supplies and stocks.
The EU’s multiple responses
Looking ahead, the EU can deploy a number of options in response, from circularity to domestic mining, including international partnerships.

Brussels is aiming to mine or recycle 10% of critical materials by 2030, but there is paradox at play here, as circularity only works once sufficient
volumes have first been mined and used. Therefore, in the coming years, international partnerships will be a priority and, for that matter, the EU
has been very active on that front since the start of the second Trump presidency.
The EU is aware of the challenges that it facesAnd the EXVI indices are particularly relevant for assessing supply chains targeted by major policy

initiatives, such as the Chips Act (strengthening the EU’s semiconductor ecosystem and technological sovereignty), the Net Zero Industry Act
(NZIA) (building domestic manufacturing capacity for clean-tech products such as solar, wind and batteries) and the Critical Raw Materials Act
(CRMA) (securing and diversifying the EU’s supply of critical and strategic raw materials). The challenges have been identified and an action plan
has been drawn up. Now it needs to be implemented on the ground.

koen.deleus@bnpparibasfortis.com
concentrated, as shown by the r elatively low Pillar 1 scor e of 0.13. However, the high Pillar 2 scor e of 0.39 shows that the EU is weak at
producing semiconductors itself. That is precisely why the European Chips Act is focusing on building domestic pr oduction capacity rather than

on diversifying imports. The same reasoning applies to raw materials and, to a lesser extent, to net-zero technologies.

## Document 330: The national accounts inkblot test (2026-09-20)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The national accounts inkblot test (2026-09-20).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-09-20
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

#australia #luciellis #bonds #housing #interestrates
The national accounts inkblot test
By Luci Ellis
Chief Economist Westpac Group, Westpac
16:30 September 04 2026 Share
A story-driven species encounters national accounts data that resist easy narratives.
In something as complex as an economy, outcomes often defy simple narratives. It is a little like
those inkblot psychology tests one sometimes sees. Is it a rabbit or is it a lady? It depends on your

perspective.

### Page 2

In something as complex as an economy, there are always many things going on, not all pointing in the
same direction. There will be headwinds and tailwinds. Most shocks and trends affect some industries
and parts of the economy more than others. The overall impact is a complicated netting exercise that is
hard to be precise about in the real world, where measurement is uncertain and things get revised.
That complexity sits uncomfortably for a species that shapes its understanding through story. A simple

cause-and-effect narrative fits the way our brains have evolved. Messy outcomes like this week’s
national accounts (and the domestic data flow more broadly) do not fit simple stories. It is tempting for
people to extract one nonetheless. The process resembles one of those old psychology tricks where
you can see a rabbit or a woman, a face or a vase, depending on your perspective, or the Rorschach
inkblot where you can “see” what you want to see. We pattern-match, we see creatures in the clouds –

and sometimes, we can be selective with data when we should not be, focusing only on what fits our
narrative.
I am not going to pretend to be above reproach on this front, being a member of the same narrative-
seeking species as everyone else. That said, I cannot help noticing how easy it is to squint at the
economic inkblot and see a hawk or a dove according to one’s prior beliefs.
Those seeing the hawk will point to (among other things) the 2.1%yr GDP growth being above the

RBA’s forecast and not below its estimate of potential output growth “speed limit”, as the RBA is trying
to engineer. They will note the tick-up in unit labour cost growth, resilient consumption, strong data
centre investment and – beyond the national accounts – the outsized July CPI inflation data. Putting
these together, a narrative forms that seemingly demands immediate further tightening of monetary
policy.
In doing so, though, one would need to leave a few things out. Among these are the fact that growth

stepped down noticeably through the year, and that about half the upside surprise in year-ended
growth was a revision to an earlier quarter, implying you got more growth for the same amount of
inflation. Also downplayed in this narrative is the weak growth in average earnings per hour – a less-
noisy input into unit labour costs – the increase in household saving and the roll-off in public sector
investment making room for higher dwelling and data centre investment.

And for those confidently expecting a September hike, it requires a belief that the RBA will go back on
their previous statement that, in this initial phase, they will focus on the quarterly inflation data rather
The national accounts for Q2 2026 were a case in point. A hawkish take would focus on GDP growth
being above RBA expectations and not below its view of trend; a dovish take would focus on the loss
of momentum more recently. Likewise a hawk could point to the outsized July CPI print, while the

dove can remind you of the noise and uncertain seasonality in the monthly data. Both narratives
inherently leave out contradicting signals.
The key issue here is to make sure that you are taking signal from all the available data, not just the
things that fit the narrative. Labour market data deserve more consideration than some observers are
giving them. Also important is remembering that the next quarter will always be thrown about by
special factors, and what matters is where things are in a year or so’s time.

### Page 3

than a new monthly series with its short history and uncertain seasonality that is prone to revision. (And
the RBA might well go back on that commitment, but one must explicitly believe it will.)
The same exercise of leaving pieces out to make a neater story also permeates the dove case. One
could highlight that the 2.1% year-ended GDP growth rate was comprised of two halves, with the most
recent showing much weaker growth in domestic demand. One can point to the larger-than-expected

downturn in the housing market, weak per capita growth, moderate consumption outside a few special
factors such as EV purchases, and the likely run of monthly trimmed mean CPI prints from here being
noticeably below the July figure.
This narrative also requires leaving a few things out, though. As well as the components of the hawk
case already noted, there is the issue that the data centre boom could well offset the receding public
sector’s release of construction resources.

Part of the difficulty is that the narratives we seek can be so black-and-white binary. It is also
embedded in the here and now, immediate data flow. We should ask ourselves whether we should
really take so much signal from small surprises in the latest data. The crucial question is what these
noisy outcomes mean for the underlying trends, and where growth and inflation will be in a year or so.
We also note that much of the recent discussion has made relatively little of the labour market. It has

been easing over much of the past year or so (contra the RBA’s initial assessment earlier this year that
it was re-tightening) and is noticeably softer than RBA expectations. Today’s labour accounts confirm
that more people have taken on secondary jobs to address cost-of-living pressures. Labour supply,
both in terms of the number of people in the labour force and the number of hours they want to work,
has been more resilient than many observers expected. The various measures of underemployment

are pointing to emerging spare capacity that might not be widely recognised.
What is the resolution, if any? Structured analysis including whole-economy models is one way through
the difficulty, but it relies on the models being a decent representation of reality. Perhaps another,
honouring the fact our brains evolved to want simple stories, is to emulate Charles Darwin and
systematically note down any piece of evidence against your current theory. It might make it harder to

tell a simple story, but it will mean you are less likely to miss something that later turns out to be
important.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS3
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMIC OUTLOOK
The June quarter National Accounts showed Australia’s
economy expanded 0.4% in Q2 and 2.1% in year-ended
terms. It was the first full quarter that households and
businesses had to deal with the global energy shock flowing

from the conflict in the Middle East and higher domestic
interest rates. With a strong ramp up in data centre
investment also coming off a massive surge in Q1, the result
was a noisy quarter. Looking through this, the Accounts
point to growth stabilising at a slower pace. The household
sector has been resilient with solid income growth
providing support, partly reflecting the upswing in business
investment, which still has further to run.
The data centre and renewables investment upswing

continues to gather momentum. Consistent with the upward
revision to surveyed capex intentions, we now expect new
business investment to grow by almost 13% in 2026 (up
from 11.7%yr previously) and a further 10% in 2027 (up from
7.5%yr). The upswing also appears to be generating larger
spillovers to related industries, including construction and
professional services, supporting incomes. The Westpac–
DataX Card Tracker suggests spending momentum
strengthened through Q2 and into Q3, with household

consumption likely to rise by 0.8%qtr in Q3 and 2.0%yr in
2026 (up from 1.7%yr previously).
Construction has also strengthened and the outlook remains
positive. Beyond the data centre and renewable energy
build-out, non-residential building is being supported by
the need to accommodate a growing population, while
residential construction continues to benefit from a range of
state and federal government initiatives. This is offsetting
the wind-down in public infrastructure spending. We expect

construction activity to remain resilient despite the headwinds
from higher interest rates, higher energy costs associated
with the Middle East conflict, rising labour costs and falling
established house prices.
What does this mean for the aggregate outlook?
Aggregate economic conditions have firmed. Business-
led activity, which appears insensitive to interest rates, is
providing the economy with a tailwind strong enough to
support broader areas of the economy that are not directly tied

to the data centre/renewables build-out.
As a result, we have upgraded our near-term outlook to 1.5%yr
in 2026, up from 1.0%yr expected prior to the Q2 National
Accounts. The outlook beyond 2026 is largely unchanged,
although the risks are tilted to the upside given the size of the
potential increase in renewable investment. We expect growth
to accelerate to 1.7%yr in 2027 and 2.5%yr in 2028 as the RBA
eases rates in the second half of 2027 in response to emerging

spare capacity.
The economy shifts to business-led growth
Pat Bustamante, Senior Economist
Reflecting the firmer near-term outlook, we have revised our
forecast peak in the unemployment rate down slightly from
4.9% (in Q1 2027) to 4.8% (in Q2 2027), where it is expected
to hold around this level throughout 2027. The slightly tighter
labour market, combined with stronger global inflationary
pressures associated with the AI investment boom, points
to a firm near-term inflation pulse of 3.3%yr in 2026 before

easing to 2.7%yr in 2027 and 2.3%yr in 2028 as spare capacity
emerges and productivity gradually improves. We now expect
the RBA Board to hike in September  2026 , with the cash rate
peaking at 4.6% before policy is cautiously eased in the second
half of 2027.

### Page 4

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
